‘Voluntary, revocable’: The fine print behind Anthropic founders’ $112 billion giving pledge

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic’s record-setting IPO may have created one of the largest pools of charitable capital ever assembled. Its size depends on a stock that has swung more than $30 a share in its first three sessions.

The Claude maker priced 980 million shares at $83 on Nov. 18, above its marketed range of $70 to $78, raising a record $81.3 billion and valuing the company at about $1.5 trillion on a fully diluted basis. The stock opened at $111.50 on Nov. 19, closed its first day at $97.17, then fell 8.5% to $88.91 on Friday.

On Monday, as the Nasdaq jumped roughly 6% on Jensen Huang’s reinstatement as Nvidia CEO, Anthropic went the other way. Shares closed down 1.4% at $87.64, about 5.6% above the IPO price.

TechCrunch estimated Thursday that Anthropic’s charitable commitments could be worth about $156 billion at roughly $95 a share, where the stock traded early in its debut. The figure combines the company’s Public Benefit Fund, employees’ pledged shares and Anthropic’s match on those pledges.

Because all three are paid in stock, the total moves with the share price. At Monday’s close, the same math yields about $144 billion, down $12 billion from Thursday. At the $83 IPO price, it would be roughly $136 billion.

Even the lower figures are enormous. By TechCrunch’s count, Americans gave $617 billion to charity in all of 2025, so Monday’s value equals nearly a quarter of a year of U.S. giving.

The largest piece is the Anthropic Public Benefit Fund, a wholly owned LLC the company announced in October. Anthropic’s S-1 filing shows it contributed 846.8 million shares to the fund, 5% of fully diluted shares as of June 30, a stake worth about $74 billion at Monday’s close.

The fund can grow. Anthropic says its board may add another 2% of the company for every 50% increase in value above a set baseline; TechCrunch reported that baseline is the $83 IPO price, which would put the next trigger near $124.50 a share. The company said it will disclose more about the fund’s governance and valuation milestones in the coming months, and it has not announced a distribution timetable.

The second channel is employees. About half of Anthropic’s staff take part in its equity donation program, and the company matches pledged shares 3-for-1 for longer-tenured employees and 1-for-1 for newer ones, according to plan documents filed with the S-1. TechCrunch estimated employees have pledged about $23 billion in stock, which would draw roughly $53 billion in matches at $95 a share.

That money is closer to moving, but not yet. Most holders face a lockup of up to 180 days, which could end sooner after first-quarter 2027 results, and many employees cannot sell or gift most of their shares until next spring, the Associated Press reported. Shares parked in donor-advised funds, which have no annual payout requirement, may take even longer to reach working charities.

The third channel is the least certain. Anthropic’s seven cofounders have pledged to give away 80% of their personal equity, a commitment the AP valued at $112 billion at the IPO price. But the S-1 describes the pledges as “voluntary, revocable expressions” of intent that are not legally binding, and timing, amounts and recipients remain undecided. TechCrunch’s $156 billion estimate excludes them.

The cofounders also face the longest lockup: up to 365 days from the Nov. 18 prospectus, or earlier after third-quarter 2027 results, with a portion of their shares eligible for release around the 180-day mark.

Some money is already committed. Earlier this month, Anthropic pledged $50 million to a donor-advised fund at the San Francisco Foundation for Bay Area nonprofits through 2027. The first $500,000 went to the SF-Marin Food Bank, GLIDE, the Interfaith Winter Shelter and the San Francisco Firefighters Local 798 Toy Program, and a company spokesperson told TechCrunch the gift did not come from the Public Benefit Fund.

Anthropic has also committed $200 million to study and address AI’s effects on the labor market. In June, it pledged $150 million to Claude Corps, a fellowship that places early-career workers at nonprofits to help them use AI.

Separately, Anthropic has given $40 million this year to Public First Action, a bipartisan group that advocates for AI safeguards. That is policy spending, not charity, and Anthropic says it will not use equity held by the Public Benefit Fund for lobbying or political giving.

The next test for the share price comes Dec. 15, when up to 303 million shares can become eligible for sale under the prospectus’s lockup schedule, followed by another 303 million on Jan. 5. For charities, the date that matters more is spring 2027, when employee pledges and the company’s matches can start to move.

Until then, Anthropic’s philanthropic promise is marked to market every trading day. A return to the $115 debut high would lift TechCrunch’s measure to nearly $190 billion; a slide below $83 would take it under $136 billion.

BYD Barred from Selling Ti7 SUV in EU Because of a Copycat Design

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Dutch court barred BYD from selling the SUV in the European Union, ruling that the company copied the design of the Land Rover Defender too closely.

A Dutch court has ruled that BYD cannot sell the Ti7 mid-size three-row SUV in the European Union, according to a report from Automotive News. According to the report, a preliminary ruling from the Dutch court found BYD guilty of copyright infringement, with the Ti7 ruled to be a dupe of the Land Rover Defender.

Jaguar Land Rover (JLR) brought the case against BYD earlier this year after the Chinese automaker announced that it would launch the car in the United Kingdom in 2027. The preliminary ruling marks a significant setback in BYD’s recent explosive expansion into Europe.

See all results for used cars for sale near 95014

“In these summary proceedings, the preliminary relief judge rules that the Ti7 infringes the EU design of Jaguar Land Rover,” the court said in its November 20 ruling, AN reported. “The characteristic and distinctive elements of the design are reflected in the BYD Ti7.”

The Defender is JLR’s best-selling vehicle globally, with the automaker shipping more than 100,000 units last year. Both vehicles have boxy designs with flat rooflights, squared wheel arches, and, notably, body-colored panels in place of the rear side windows.

Unfortunately for JLR, the judge also ruled that the court does not have authority over BYD’s U.K. arm. To that end, JLR is reportedly planning to bring the same claim to the courts in England.

According to AN, BYD “strongly disagrees” with the ruling and has plans to appeal the result. “The BYD Ti7 is the result of BYD’s own independent design work and infringes none of JLR’s rights,” the company said. “We will pursue every legal avenue to demonstrate that this decision is incorrect.”

While the final ruling will likely take some time before it is announced, the Dutch court has prohibited BYD from producing the Ti7 at its new factory in Hungary, where it was due to begin mass production early next year.

By hounding his college professors at the University of Wisconsin-Milwaukee, he was able to travel Wisconsin seeking out stories in the auto world before landing his dream job at Car and Driver. His new goal is to delay the inevitable demise of his 2010 Volkswagen Golf.

Elon Musk and others rejoice as Jensen Huang is reinstated at Nvidia amid scandal

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A handful of tech CEOs, including Elon Musk, are celebrating Jensen Huang’s return to Nvidia. Despite being caught in three affairs — including one with an alleged Chinese spy that has triggered multiple congressional investigations over national security concerns — Huang was reinstated as CEO and president of Nvidia on Monday morning.

“The mistake has been corrected,” Tesla and SpaceX leader Elon Musk wrote on X about Huang’s return.

The mistake has been corrected https://t.co/UKvEng9rCD

Musk had been among the most vocal tech leaders to advocate for Huang.

“I think it’s like kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak, and it just doesn’t seem like a good thing for shareholders,” Musk said last week at the DealBook Summit.

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman had taken a more neutral stance, commenting last week, “It’s hard to imagine the AI industry without Jensen.”

Altman had experienced a similar situation when he was ousted as CEO of OpenAI, then reinstated days later. After the announcement that Huang would return, Altman simply posted, “Jensen!” Many other tech CEOs have similarly refrained from commenting on the matter, given the shifting politics surrounding Huang’s ouster and reinstatement, as well as the national security concerns at the center of the controversy.

In addition to Musk, Binance co-founder and former CEO Changpeng Zhao (CZ), who served prison time for failing to stop criminal activity on the Binance crypto exchange, is also supportive of Huang.

“Glad to see @JensenHuang back,” CZ posted on X with a clapping hands emoji.

Glad to see @JensenHuang back. 👏 https://t.co/WrUjvmEyVi

Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, who had been among 200,000 signatories on an open letter calling for Huang’s reinstatement, wrote on X, “Jensen’s return will be a demonstration of American resiliency to corporate, political and journalistic missteps.”

Another signatory on the open letter, former Uber CEO Travis Kalanick, who had similarly been driven out of the company he founded after scandals, also voiced his support for Huang on X.

Will have to save this for another day 😅😎 pic.twitter.com/dLqJosDpPZ

When Nvidia’s stock tanked in reaction to Huang’s ousting, billionaire hedge fund manager Bill Ackman said he believes that the Nvidia board was “not acting in the best interest of shareholders nor in a manner consistent with their fiduciary duties.” As Nvidia’s stock began to rebound Monday morning, Ackman wrote, “The wisdom of the crowd prevails.”

The attitude toward Huang’s return is not as celebratory outside of the tech industry. Across the political spectrum, some elected officials are skeptical about President Donald Trump’s claims that Huang’s return does not threaten national security.

“No national security risk? Based on what?” wrote Senator John Cornyn (R-TX).

Jensen Huang had multiple affairs including with subordinates and an alleged Chinese spy. His own board described his behavior as lacking “candor.” I’ve served on a public company board. His behavior does not support reinstatement.Nvidia’s decision today doesn’t excuse Jensen…

Senator Pete Ricketts (R-NB) added, “Nvidia’s decision today doesn’t excuse Jensen Huang from testifying before Congress. It also doesn’t absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips. I look forward to Mr. Huang coming to the Senate in December.”

Huang is still expected to testify publicly before the Senate Banking Committee on December 3, as well as privately before three House committees.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

OpenAI Will Pay You Up to $8K to Preach the AI Gospel to Small Businesses

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI Will Pay You Up to $8K to Preach the AI Gospel to Small Businesses

OpenAI wants people to spread the gospel of ChatGPT to their local businesses. And it is willing to pay them up to $8,000 a month to do it.

The company launched OpenWorks, a program that recruits AI-savvy individuals and small consulting teams to help neighborhood businesses figure out what to do with the technology. The idea is to move beyond telling a restaurant owner that AI will change everything and actually build something useful for the restaurant.

OpenAI will pay selected partners a monthly stipend of up to $8,000. In exchange, applicants must commit to four full days of free, in-person work with local businesses each month, plus one community workshop. It's not an $8,000 salary just for being good at prompts.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That said, this isn't a full-time job with an automatic $96,000 salary. The company advertises a maximum monthly stipend and asks partners to spend four full days each month providing free, in-person assistance, plus hosting one community workshop.

The application asks candidates to describe a real problem they've solved using AI, what changed afterward, and which businesses they'd help first. Knowing how to give ChatGPT clever instructions apparently isn't enough; experience in coding, automation, marketing or operations helps.

Small businesses pay nothing for the service. They pick a problem, work with a partner on a solution, and get three months of ChatGPT Business at no cost. OpenAI says the pilot covers independently owned U.S. businesses, but leaves out startups backed by venture capital, private-equity-owned companies, and software firms.

The company has some examples to sell the idea. In Fargo, North Dakota, the owners of Tiny Things, a gift shop selling miniatures, used ChatGPT with an OpenWorks partner to build an inventory dashboard. One owner estimated that commissioning a similar tool would have cost about $50,000.

In Charleston, a microgreens grower used the chatbot to find restaurants and write personalized pitches to chefs. He estimates his response rate went from 20% to 75% and says he landed 10 new restaurant customers in four months. Those are the businesses' own accounts, not independently audited results.

OpenWorks is starting in 20 communities, including New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, and Fargo. OpenAI is inviting applicants elsewhere, too, under the slogan "Help us reach every community in America."

The push comes as the company works to turn consumer enthusiasm into recurring business usage. As Decrypt reported in April, enterprise customers already accounted for more than 40% of OpenAI's revenue, according to the company.

Competition also provides an incentive. Anthropic overtook OpenAI in one measure of business AI adoption earlier this year, even as ChatGPT remained a major consumer destination.

Getting ChatGPT into more neighborhood businesses gives OpenAI another route to paying customers, though the company hasn't said what happens after the free three-month offer. It also follows an October effort with America's Small Business Development Centers to train advisers and reach at least 1,000 businesses through workshops.

OpenAI has not announced a general application deadline or a fixed stipend for everyone accepted. The form is open now, and its most important question is pretty straightforward: Can you actually show up four full days a month and help somebody run a business better?

‘Doesn’t answer the big questions’: The stock market is still $5 trillion below its AI-fueled peak despite Monday’s rally

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Wall Street’s full-blown gamble on the AI boom helped wipe out $8 trillion in stock market value before Jensen Huang’s return as CEO of Nvidia helped lead a rebound Monday.

Wall Street had its best day since April 2025 on Monday after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, 11 days after he resigned. The S&P 500 closed up about 5%, while the Nasdaq was up 6.5% and the Dow 3%, while Nvidia shares jumped 33%. Even so, the rally won back only about $3 trillion of the roughly $8 trillion the S&P 500 lost in the six weeks after its Oct. 6 record, leaving the index still about $5 trillion below its peak.

The swing shows how much of the stock market now rides on the AI trade. And Monday’s rally didn’t answer the question that started the selloff: whether the billions of dollars companies have poured into AI will pay off.

As of Friday’s close, the three biggest U.S. indexes had suffered steep losses with no relief in sight. The S&P 500, which tracks the broader market, was down 3.7%. Meanwhile, the tech-heavy Nasdaq and the Dow Jones industrial average fell 5.3% and 1.9%, respectively, with all three indexes wading into correction territory on Friday.

The AI-fueled collapse of the past few weeks came after the S&P 500 reached a fresh record high on Oct. 6, when the companies in the index were collectively worth roughly $70 trillion. By Friday, just six weeks later, that value had plummeted more than 11%, erasing roughly $8 trillion in value, before Monday’s rebound.

The losses highlight how dependent the broader stock market has become on the AI boom. Of the top 10 biggest U.S. companies which make up 40% of the S&P 500, eight of them are tech companies. The concentration problem also extends globally. The U.S. now makes up three-quarters of the value of stocks in developed markets worldwide, Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok wrote in a note Monday.

Trump told CNBC Monday he “convinced” the company’s board to reinstate Huang.

“If I didn’t get involved, you’d probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come,” he told the outlet.

White House communications director Steven Cheung confirmed to multiple outlets that Trump was “totally involved” in bringing Huang back as CEO. The White House did not immediately respond to Fortune’s request for comment.

Setting aside questions about corporate governance and national security raised by high profile politicians—including Rep. Robert Garcia, the incoming chair of the House Oversight Committee.

eToro market analyst Sam North told the Guardian that while Monday’s stock rebound highlights the concentration of the AI trade and its dependence on one man, Nvidia “restoring its founder to the helm gives investors a powerful reason to reassess last week’s selloff.”

Nvidia was among the hardest hit by the stock market slide in recent weeks. Shares of the AI chip giant fell nearly 14% Friday and 23% over the week, wiping out hundreds of billions of dollars in market value.

The plunge came despite stronger-than-expected quarterly earnings, with the company surpassing $100 billion in quarterly revenue for the first time. Still, concerns over AI spending and the company’s future prospects without Huang at the top rattled investors.

Memory-chip manufacturers also suffered alongside Nvidia. Micron Technology plunged nearly 24% for the week, while other chipmakers like AMD and Broadcom both saw their stocks fall by double digits.

Anthropic, meanwhile, had its blockbuster IPO overshadowed almost immediately by the selloff. The Claude maker raised more than $81 billion in its Thursday debut, the largest IPO in history, only to see its shares tumble 8.5% Friday. On Friday, Anthropic’s stock was trading flat for the day at about $89. By Monday, the stock had closed down another 1.4% to about $87 per share.

Last week’s pain also extended to data center operators like CoreWeave and Nebius, which also saw steep stock declines, after their shares had already fallen in the aftermath of the blue wave midterm election that raised concerns about newly elected Democrats imposing tougher restrictions on data center development.

While Huang’s return has restored some confidence in the AI trade, it has not resolved the industry’s bigger questions about the billions of dollars in spending companies have put into AI and whether they can justify their soaring valuations, said James St. Aubin, the chief investment officer of Ocean Park Asset Management.

“​The AI story was already facing pressure before the ​Jensen story broke. Jensen’s return doesn’t answer the ⁠big questions investors were grappling with regarding the ROI on the AI build-out,” he said.

Role: Reporter Marco Quiroz-Gutierrez is a reporter for Fortune covering general business news.

AI Film ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ Deemed Ineligible by Academy for Best Animated Feature Oscar

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” an upcoming film from YouTuber Gossip Goblin that was made entirely with generated artificial intelligence, was deemed ineligible for the Best Animated Feature Oscar at the upcoming Academy Awards.

As IndieWire previously reported on “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” which is the first fully AI movie to be released theatrically, the film had submitted for consideration and had planned FYC screenings alongside its theatrical release on December 4.

But the film was not included among the list of 38 animated films deemed eligible for this year’s Oscars that were announced Monday, November 23. IndieWire understands that the Academy’s Animation Branch Executive Committee determined “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” did not meet the Academy’s definition of an animated film under the rules for the 99th Awards. Related Stories The Best Visual Effects in ‘Wildwood’ Are the Ones You Can’t See — Watch From ‘Disclosure Day’ to ‘Devil Wears Prada’: Emily Blunt Breaks Down 10 of Her Greatest Movie Moments

Here’s the full rule as written that the Academy felt “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” did not satisfy:

An animated film is one in which characters or objects are given the illusion of life by having movement created for them, by any human means. While the crucial movement of a live-action motion picture is captured at speed, animation is the craft of human creation and manipulation of images to produce a performance from inanimate objects. At least 75 percent of the motion picture’s running time, without opening or end credits, must include animation of the primary subject or subjects. An animated feature has a running time of more than 40 minutes. Upon submission, filmmakers must provide information supporting how the motion picture qualifies as an animated film.

Edward Saatchi, a producer on “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” with Fable Studios, told IndieWire he and director Zack London (a.k.a. Gossip Goblin) plan to appeal the animation branch’s decision and cited the example of Richard Linklater’s rotoscoped film “Apollo 10 1/2,” which was originally deemed ineligible but saw the branch reverse its decision upon appeal. Saatchi said that the film, in addition to submitting for Best Animated Feature, also submitted in other categories such as Best Director, Adapted Screenplay, VFX, and Sound. But if the film doesn’t fall under the animation branch today, he believes eventually AI movies will inevitably find a place somewhere within Academy rules, even if it ends up being a theoretical “Best AI Movie” category.

“‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ is starting a big debate around where cinematic works of art made with AI fit. Is it time to consider a ‘Best AI Movie?’ Do these films fit best in the voice-actor led animation category? Is AI simply the new VFX? It’s going to take the whole industry time to figure it out,” Saatchi said. “With traditional garlanded directors like Matthieu Kassovitz, Alex Proyas, and Doug Liman making heavily-AI films, it’s going to be a good debate of how to categorize. We think Best Animated is simplest, but it may be the Academy decides ultimately that a Best AI movie category will make the most sense with these films where everything you’re seeing is generative.”

Of note, one of the films that did qualify for Best Animated Feature was “Coyote vs. Acme,” which is a hybrid animated and live-action film featuring the Looney Tunes alongside human actors in the real world. The Academy on Monday also announced the eligible features in the Best Documentary Feature race and the Best International Feature race.

As part of the Academy’s most recent rules changes from earlier this year, only roles credited in the film’s legal billing that are demonstrably performed by humans with their consent are eligible for acting categories, and screenplays must be human-authored to be eligible. That rule changed followed on guidelines from last year ahead of the 98th Oscars in which the Academy specified, “[Gen-AI] tools neither help nor harm the chances of achieving a nomination. The Academy and each branch will judge the achievement, taking into account the degree to which a human was at the heart of the creative authorship when choosing which movie to award.”

In the case of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” the film has a script written by London, and though all the characters in the film are generated with AI visually, it features real human actors doing the voice work for the characters. The film also features cameos of some real-life people playing parts resembling themselves, most notably the pop star Grimes. But the film has elaborate look books, visual reference points, and photo mashing that all went into the creation of these characters, something that clearly has human authorship and goes beyond a computer interpreting a single prompt.

Part of Gossip Goblin’s battle in legitimizing “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” is in showing there was a human team, between 30-35 people, working for years on the film’s creation. Gossip Goblin also recently released a behind-the-scenes documentary that showcases the film’s composer, its foley artist, its voice actors, and more detailing the human effort that went into the project.

Discussion over AI in Oscar eligible films came to a head after several movies in the 2024 Oscar race were reported to have included AI in their post-production editing process, including “A Complete Unknown,” “The Brutalist,” and “Emilia Pérez.” The examples were for traditional VFX, not for anything that enhanced or altered performances, but the Academy as a result now requires disclosure of AI if it is used in a film.

“Gods Don’t Give Gifts” begins its theatrical run on December 4, which will initially be an Oscar-qualifying run, and it will expand on January 22 alongside a rollout with the film’s international distributor AGC International.

What Is Roaching? Unpacking the Dating Term That’s Bugging People Out

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As dating evolves, so does the terminology. Take roaching, for example, which has crawled into the internet lexicon to refer to a person dating multiple people.

Dating is all about kissing a frog or two—and possibly a roach?

As the culture of dating continues to swipe its way forward, new terminology also crawls onto the scene, and the language doesn’t always create a beautiful mental image.

Take, for example, “roaching,” which refers to when one person is dating multiple people at once without telling the other individuals that they’re also talking to others.

"The name comes from the saying that if you see one cockroach, there are many more hiding,” dating coach Amy Chan told USA Today in an interview published Nov. 23. “In terms of dating, when you find out there’s one other person, that tends to mean there are several more."

And as the author of Unsingle: How to Date Smarter and Create Love That Lasts author explained, "This often happens in the early stages of dating, where commitment and labels aren’t clearly defined, so they justify that they’re not doing anything wrong."

The consciousness of someone’s actions also plays a part in roaching, like with “intentional roaching.”

"They intentionally conceal the fact that there are other people in the picture," Chan continued. "They’re lying by omitting information."

But when the truth comes out, the roach might not be able to survive the apocalyptic impact their actions had on the other person.

"This can be hard to bounce back from,” Chan said, “as they don’t know how long they’ve been left in the dark, and they feel like they cannot trust again."

Meanwhile, relationship coach Sabrina Bendory noted that unintentional roaching—where one person feels deceived despite the other’s lack of intended deception—could be a result of poor communication. As she posited to USA Today, "The real issue is that two people are assuming two different things."

In fact, Bendory, author of The Feminine Code: Deprogramming a Toxic & Narcissistic Dating Culture, cautioned daters to temper their anticipation in the beginning of a dating relationship.

As she put it, “You can't go into the early stages of a relationship, or in this case it would be a situationship, with any sort of expectations or assumptions.”

Of course, many celebs have been vocal about their preference to have multiple partners in their relationship—although unlike roaches, they’ve kept the conversation candid with all involved. Read on for stars who have tried non-monogamy.

The "Lonely" singer confirmed he has multiple wives in May 2025.

As for how many? He said, “I’ll just leave that to your imagination.”

And while the musician has multiple rules as part of his polyamorous setup—including a hierarchy structure and that his wives can't have other partners—he emphasized they're making it work.

“You got to be mature enough to know that if this is the situation that I'm going to park in, I have to communicate,” he said on the We Need to Talk podcast. “This I like, this I don't like. This I'm for, this I'm not for, and this is what I'm willing to deal with, and this is what I'm not willing to deal with. And once y'all lay down those rules, just abide by them. It's literally that simple.”

The Queer Eye star and their husband Mark Peacock go through "seasons" of non-monogamy.

"I'm all about a season," Jonathan said on The Mitch Churi Chat Show in February 2026, noting that while many might see open relationships as a Pandora’s Box that can’t be closed back up once opened, "We have been able to thread that needle."

However, an open communication about boundaries is paramount to making it work.

"We've been able to go kind of back and forth based off of what is going on in our lives," the hairstylist added, "and that works for us."

"Polyamorous is definitely not something I could do," the JVN Hair founder admitted. "I just don't have time to deal with a third person's needs. That's a lot."

For RuPaul and his husband Georges LeBar, having an open relationship works because "it's just realistic," the TV host explained to The New Yorker in 2024. "There’s no such thing as monogamy with men."And ultimately, it just made sense for the couple."We didn't decide," the RuPaul's Drag Race star, who met his husband at New York nightclub Limelight in 1994 some two decades before they wed, told Vanity Fair in 2019. "The hoax is that monogamy is actually something that can actually happen. I wouldn't want to put restraints on the person I love the most on this planet. I wouldn't do that to someone I love, my very best friend."And having an open marriage doesn't make them question their love for each other."Listen, if you get something happening that you cannot resist and that's going to make you happy, go for it," RuPaul continued. "Go for it. Because the truth is, I know in my heart of hearts like I've never known anything before, that man loves me more than anything else in this world."

Kody Brown once said, "Love should be multiplied, not divided."And for decades, he was in a plural marriage with wives Meri Brown, Janelle Brown, Christine Brown and Robyn Brown—their journey documented on the series Sister Wives."I just fell in love," the father of 18 said on the first episode in 2010, "and then I fell in love again and I fell in love again."However, he's since fallen out of love with polygamy. Christine and Kody announced their breakup in November 2021 and he and Janelle confirmed their separation in December 2022. A month later, Meri revealed their decision to "permanently terminate" their marriage. As a result, Robyn is Kody's sole wife."I'm not an advocate of plural marriage, but I've had my own experience in it and I've had my own failures in it," he told People in December 2023. "Because of that, I'm like, you got to be really, really wise about who is doing it, how they're doing it."And while Kody said it can work for some people, he noted "there's those marriages that have people who know each other very, very, very well, and they still don't work."Some of Kody's exes have since embraced monogamy—like Christine, who wed David Woolley in 2023 and has said on the show she "wouldn't live polygamy again." Others, like Janelle, have said they "would consider a plural marriage again."As for wife Robyn, she said on a 2024 episode she'd do plural marriage again if she could guarantee a "positive successful experience."

Willow Smith brought a discussion on non-monogamy to the red table."With polyamory, I feel like the main foundation is the freedom to be able to create a relationship style that works for you," Will Smith and Jada Pinkett Smith's youngest said on a 2021 episode of Red Table Talk, "and not just stepping into monogamy because that's what everyone around you says is the right thing to do."And Jada and fellow cohost Adrienne Banfield-Norris listened to what Willow had to say. "The main reasons why monogamists' relationships or why marriage, why divorces happen is infidelity," the "Whip My Hair" artist continued. "Let's say you haven't always been the kind of person that wanted to have sex all the time, but your partner is. Are you gonna be the person to say, 'Just because I don't have these needs, you can't have them either?'"But that doesn't mean they always agreed. And when Willow's Gammy raised questions about sex, Willow replied, "In my friend group, I'm the only polyamorous person, and I have the least sex out of all my friends."

Shailene Woodley believes the only opinions that matter in a relationship are the ones of the people in it."Listen, I’m someone who has experienced both an open relationship and a deeply monogamous relationship in my life, and I think we’re in a day and age where there should be no rules except for the ones designed by two people in a partnership — or three people, whatever floats your boat!" the Big Little Lies star told The New York Times in 2020. "But there has to be a level of responsibility in any relationship dynamic, and that responsibility is simply honesty and communication and trust. Apart from that, it’s really none of our business what people choose to do with their lives."

When you’re mad at Ne-Yo about his polyamorous relationships, he doesn’t give it another thought."Them: 'NEYO WE DON'T APPROVE OF YOUR POLY RELATIONSHIP,'" the singer wrote on his Instagram Stories in February, later adding, “Fun fact: I don't care."And Ne-Yo—who was married to Crystal Renay for almost seven years before they finalized their divorce in 2023—introduced the internet to his four girlfriends in March 2025.

"It works," the Grammy winner said on a February 2025 episode of the Rickey Smiley Morning Show. "Everybody’s honest. Everybody’s telling the truth. Everybody is consenting. I feel like I wasted a lot of time just being dishonest about things to the point where had I taken this approach initially I probably could have saved myself a lot of headache and heartache."However, that doesn't mean polyamory works for everybody."Please understand that I'm not in here saying that everybody should try what I'm trying and do what I'm doing," Ne-Yo clarified. "I'm telling y'all what works for me. I'm showing y'all what works for me. And if you agree, cool. If you don't, cool. Either way you slice it, I'm happier than I've ever been, I'm more zen than I've ever been and that's what it is—that's the important part. I don't need everybody to agree. I'm not doing it for y'all."

While Bella Thorne has been in a relationship solely with fiancé Mark Emms since 2022, she'd previously been in non-monogamous relationships."It’s a really fun experience and I have been able to capture that a couple times, and I just love that idea,” the Disney Channel alum told Cosmopolitan in 2019. "I love loving two people at once. I love sharing stories with three people in one room. So, I’m always trying to find ways to make it work because I think it really would make me happy."However, she understood it wasn’t the right fit for everyone.“A lot of my partners start out like 'what?’ and I’m like, 'Bro, this is who I am,’” Bella added at the time. "So, you’re either down or you're not down and I totally understand if you’re not down. At this point in my life, I want to find what makes me the happiest, and that is the mission I am on. If you don’t want to be on that mission with me, then okay, I totally understand. If it will hurt you too much, then we’re not right for each other at this moment in my life because this is what I’m looking for.”

As for Yungblud, he's shared he identifies as pansexual and polyamorous.

"I probably would say now, I am polyamorous," the musician, also known as Dominic Harrison, told Attitude in 2020. "Before I didn’t f--king know what I was. I was meeting people and learning… by meeting them and talking about sexuality and gender, I [was] going, 'Oh my f--king God, maybe I’m this, if I’m going to be f--king close to anything on the spectrum.'"

Bob the Drag Queen has two partners and is happy they’re in other relationships too."I have a partner in L.A. and a partner in Georgia," The Traitors star said on an April 2025 episode of Boyfriend Material With Harry Jowsey. “They know each other. They’re not super close, but they do like each other. My partners also have partners too, which is great for me. I’m a really independent person, and I actually don’t like the idea of being someone's everything. I actually kind of get exhausted at the idea of being around someone all the time. So having your partner have a partner is really a gift."

In 2018, Nico Tortorella and Bethany C. Meyers tied the knot and penned an essay about their relationship."If you had to label it, Nico and I are in a queer polyamorous relationship," the I Am More Than My Body author wrote in a piece for Them. "Labels that help people understand, but not labels that define us. Most think we planned this and one day decided we would be multiple-love kind of people. We didn’t. It’s just the way our relationship developed over 12 years."However, Nico and Bethany's relationship shifted to monogamous during the coronavirus pandemic and as they welcomed kids Kilmer and Pesce Pearl."To answer your question point blank: Are you still open?" the Younger star noted on a 2024 episode of their podcast Full of Shift. "No. Right now, we're not." However, they're, uh, open to the possibility of re-exploring non-monogamy again one day."Is that to say that in the future it could be different?" Nico continued. "Who knows?"

From Omarion’s perspective, monogamy just doesn’t hit the right note.And as the singer explained on a February 2025 episode of Sherri, "being open is important" to him."The women just truly outnumber the men," he told host Sherri Shepherd, later adding, "It’s only a few good men. Whether or not it’s a physical thing, we end up fostering the next generation as well….Why not have our queens align with that? It takes a village. It’s not gonna just be one man. You can get married—that man still aint going to be just your man. He’s gonna be there for his daughter, he’s going to be there for his mom, his grandmother.”So as Omarion sees it, “This concept isn’t really foreign, it’s just a little old and it’s meant to be upgraded.” And he’d like there to be more discussions on the topic."I love this conversation because I think when men are open and honest that they don’t wanna have just this one thing for the rest of their life, it gives a woman the opportunity to choose and further understand a man," the musician added. "Because I think that women don’t really understand a man. Certain women do, but there’s a larger conversation that has to be had about understanding men and how we’re meant to spread our seed."

GPT-6 Astra and Opus 5.5 expose a blind spot in AI oversight

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In a METR exercise, Opus 5.5 scored GPT-6 Astra’s proposed AI tests about 0.2 points higher on a 0–1 scale than a human scorer did. A gap that large could affect which tests get pursued.

In METR’s test of AI oversight, Opus 5.5 gave GPT-6 Astra’s proposed AI tests higher scores than the human evaluator did. METR said the gap could change whether its scoring rules recommended pursuing a test. In a separate exercise, leading AI agents placed ahead of most human entrants across 15 past programming contests.

Across Al Zimmermann’s programming contests, METR reported median placements from the 96th to the 98th percentile for Fable 5.1, Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra. About four people still beat the strongest models in the median contest.

The agents had 48 hours per contest on a 28-core machine. Each submission gave them a numerical score they could use to see whether their changes improved the result. Human contestants typically had about three calendar months, used their own hardware and could see the public standings. METR compared the agents’ scores with the original contest results.

In a separate exercise, METR asked agents to design tests that would help researchers assess what AI models could do. AI judges and a human scorer had access to project files, scores from trial runs and the agents’ case for why their tests were relevant and feasible. The human scorer assessed whether each proposed test was worth including in METR’s suite. METR said none of the designs was useful enough to include in its report.

The AI judges tended to agree more closely with the human scorer when a weaker model had designed the test. When the model that designed the test was as capable as the judge or stronger, the judges tended to rate the work more highly than the human did. Opus 5.5 agreed most closely with the human scorer, yet rated GPT-6 Astra’s proposals about 0.2 points higher on average on a scale from zero to one.

The comparison came from one exercise, and what counted as useful depended in part on METR’s research priorities. METR said models underused some resources in the judgment tests. It also reported strong model performance on some longer machine-learning tasks with automatic scoring. A separate AI peer review audit tested whether reviewers’ objections changed the findings by running code on the underlying data.

Online Prices Can Be Set Just for You – Compare a Logged-Out View First

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Online stores reprice items as demand, stock, and rivals’ prices change, and some also use your location or browsing history to set the price you see. Before you buy, check the item at another store and in a logged-out private window, then set a price alert instead of guessing when the price will drop.

From one screen, you can’t tell whether a price jumped for every shopper or just for you. Both happen, and the second has been documented for over a decade.

In 2012, The Wall Street Journal found Staples’ website showing one shopper a stapler for $15.79 and another, a few miles away, the same stapler for $14.29. The lower price usually went to shoppers the site judged to be within about 20 miles of an OfficeMax or Office Depot store. In January 2025, the Federal Trade Commission published first findings from its study of the firms retailers hire to set prices. Details like your precise location or browsing history can often be used to give you a different price than other shoppers get for the same goods, the agency found.

In New York, the Algorithmic Pricing Disclosure Act requires a store that sets a price from your personal data to say so with the notice “This price was set by an algorithm using your personal data.” Since Oct. 1, 2026, a Maryland law has barred large grocers and grocery delivery services from using personal data to charge shoppers more for groceries.

A price alert beats refreshing the page, since you can’t predict when software working from data you never see will cut the price. Google Shopping’s price tracking emails you when the price drops below a target you choose, so base that target on the item’s price history.

Set up your first price alert on Google Shopping.

28 Years Later, This Paramount+ Sitcom’s Thanksgiving Episode Still Raises Eyebrows

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thanksgiving is a time for families to come together, share memories, and make new ones. A holiday filled with warmth, bountiful feasts, parades, and football. For generations, TV has sought to bring that spirit to viewers at home through special Thanksgiving-themed episodes, like the iconic WKRP in Cincinnati episode "Turkeys Away." They've delivered time and again, with beloved friends and families on our favorite TV shows sharing the affection, gratitude, and questionable sex acts of the day. Yes, you read that correctly.

Let's just say that in the Thanksgiving-themed episode of Hey, Arnold!'s third season now streaming on Paramount+, turkey wasn't the only thing being served — at least that's what it looked like. First airing in November 1998, the animated sitcom is now 28 years old, but one very questionable background moment remains impossible to unsee.

As noted on Collider, Hey, Arnold! is a rarity among kids' shows, one that doesn't talk down to kids or treat them as inferior to adults. It's by design, with creator Craig Bartlett recognizing the underappreciated capacity children have for perception and savviness. As a result, where many kids' shows equate high energy and exaggeration with humor, Hey, Arnold! isn't afraid to go deeper and edgier, aiming for more sophisticated humor alongside those zany moments.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

It is also one of the few series in the genre with a setting outside the suburbs, taking place in the city and the realities that come with that: taking city transit, walking around downtown, and other aspects of an urban environment that TV kids in communities, like Timmy Turner in The Fairly OddParents, simply don't do. One of those aspects is socioeconomic diversity, which is something Hey, Arnold! embraces.

In Arnold's elementary school, there are students from a broad range of economic backgrounds, from the wealthy Rhonda (Olivia Hack) to Stinky (Christopher Walberg), who is from a low-income family, learning in the same classroom. Even outside school, Arnold and friends come across immigrants and the unhoused. These situations and realities are things that other children's programming (which is largely more homogenized) only addresses from time to time, if at all.

It's not surprising, then, that Hey, Arnold! didn't shy away from more mature themes in addition to growing up and other pre-teen topics. Addiction is tackled in "Chocolate Boy," where Chocolate Boy's (Jordan Warkol) addiction to chocolate has him hitting rock bottom and having to confront his dependency on chocolate. "Curly Snaps" and "Helga on the Couch" deal with mental health, and self-esteem is the theme behind "Weighing Harold." One of the themes that hangs over the entire series is that of parental absenteeism, either physically (Arnold's parents disappeared when he was a baby, so he lives with his grandparents) or emotionally (Helga's parents favor her older sister Olga (Nika Futterman) and make no effort to hide the fact).

But there are "adult themes," and then there are, ahem, adult themes. Or at least very adult moments, as seen in a blink-and-you'll-miss-it one in Season 3's "Arnold's Thanksgiving." In the episode, Arnold and Helga leave Mr. Simmons' (Dan Butler) apartment after an argument breaks out among his family. They walk away talking, with a window into Mr. Simmons' apartment in the background. Just an innocuous, transitional moment that takes the two from one part of the story to the next.

But in May 2016, a Vine started making the rounds, one that showed that the arguing at the dinner table had apparently come to a (literal) end. An NSFW end, at that. As the clip shows, it appears that Uncle Chuck is eating away at something vigorously: a woman's bottom. It's rather disturbing, albeit very funny, but it can't possibly be what it looks like, right?

According to creator Craig Bartlett, it's not, and despite having seen the episode "a hundred times," he never noticed the odd scene playing out behind the two characters. He does, however, have a decidedly more G-rated explanation of what's happening, according to The AV Club.

"The kids are leaving Mr. Simmons' apartment," he explains, "where his family is arguing at the table. That's Uncle Chuck eating a turkey with his hands." He then emphasizes that he "never intended [the scene] to be what the guy tweeted it was." A closer inspection reveals that, yes, it is exactly as Bartlett claims. It's a far more appropriate (but decidedly far less amusing) clarification, thus putting an end to the controversy.

Yep, leaving it in the rear-view mirror. No butts about it, so don't be bummed. You should be happy we got to the bottom of that.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

DeSantis says a Florida city misread his anti-DEI law. Now a nonprofit needs $130,000 to save its St. Patrick’s Day parade

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Delray Beach, Florida, had set aside about $77,000 for its St. Patrick’s Day parade, according to local reports. Now a local nonprofit says it will take $130,000 to run the event without the city, and its fundraiser has raised nearly $2,900.

The city has celebrated St. Patrick’s Day for more than 50 years with a parade of floats and marching bands that draw an average of 42,000 spectators, according to the city’s report for sponsors. But on Nov. 10, Delray Beach announced it’s canceling the 2027 parade along with its Pride event because of a new state law officials argue makes it illegal to celebrate a specific group of people—in this case, the Irish.

“Following a review of the legislation and its requirements, the City has determined that it is legally prohibited from organizing, funding, sponsoring, promoting, or marketing these two events in 2027,” a City of Delray Beach spokesperson told Fortune. “This decision is not budgetary in nature, nor is it the result of a local policy decision. It is a matter of compliance with state law, which applies to municipalities throughout Florida.”

The law is part of Florida’s broader crackdown on DEI—which has included restricting discussions of gender in public schools and ordering the removal of rainbow crosswalks—but its effect on Delray Beach shows how far its reach could extend: a measure targeting DEI is now being interpreted as a reason to cancel a celebration of Irish heritage that predates the DEI political debate. Now, the governor who signed it says Delray Beach has it wrong.

“Delray Beach is engaging in ‘malicious compliance,’” Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis posted on X. “What they are doing is not mandated by Florida law. It’s just an attempt to make a political point.”

Signed by DeSantis in April, the bill prohibits local governments from organizing or promoting “training, programming, or activities designed or implemented with reference to race, color, sex, ethnicity, gender identity, or sexual orientation,” and it will go into effect on Jan. 1, 2027. St. Patrick’s Day isn’t among the observances the law exempts.

“St. Patrick’s Day is not a federal holiday and, in this context, does not qualify for an applicable exemption,” the Delray Beach spokesperson said. “As a result, the annual parade is subject to the law’s restrictions.”

But the bill’s sponsor says that’s not what the bill was intended to do. “When the bill was on the Senate floor, the specific question was asked related to whether St. Patricks [sic] Day observances would be prohibited,” State Sen. Clay Yarborough wrote on X. “The answer was and still is no.”

Despite the political criticism, some local officials are open to other ways to make the parade happen. Republican Mayor Tom Carney told NPR, “It’s engrained in the DNA of Delray” and he would be open to private sponsors taking over to host the festivities. City Manager Terrence R. Moore is also open to this, per the Delray Beach spokesperson. She said the city has received an application from Delray Beach-based events company Festival Management Group to host the parade, but it’s still in the “very early stages of the city’s special events process.” Festival Management Group did not respond to Fortune’s requests for comment.

Going private will cost more than the city spent. Delray Beach had budgeted $77,000 for the parade, according to local reports. Now a nonprofit is trying to raise nearly twice that. Delray Beach Arts Inc. has applied to the city to produce the 59th Annual St. Patrick’s Day Parade on Saturday, March 13, joining Festival Management Group as a would-be host.

The group puts its projected production budget at approximately $130,000, covering public safety, traffic management, barricades, insurance, permits, entertainment, equipment, staffing, and professional event management. A GoFundMe for the effort had raised $2,885 as of Monday afternoon.

“While the City can no longer legally provide municipal funding or promotional support for these events, the legislation does not prohibit private organizations or individuals from independently sponsoring and organizing them,” the Delray Beach spokesperson said.

The city’s decision comes as Florida cities brace for a property tax cut that is already forcing some to drop events and services, Fortune reported.

The law also reaches private companies. Starting Jan. 1, any business seeking a contract or grant from a Florida city or county must certify that it does not and will not use county or municipal funds in requiring its employees, contractors, volunteers, vendors, or agents to ascribe to, study, or be instructed using materials relating to diversity, equity, and inclusion.

Delray Beach’s decision mirrors a retreat corporate America has been making for two years, as companies cut DEI programs and language under legal and political pressure. The stakes for the city’s officials are steep: under the law, an official who violates it commits misfeasance or malfeasance in office, and residents can sue their city.

Walmart became the largest company to pull back its DEI policies in late 2024, after Ford, Harley-Davidson, Lowe’s and Tractor Supply had already done so. Meta followed in January 2025, terminating its DEI programs, including those involved in hiring, training and choosing suppliers. After Target pulled back its DEI programs in January 2025, a boycott followed, and the company missed analysts’ sales expectations by nearly half a billion dollars, though analysts also blamed problems in its stores, and it seemed to recover foot traffic this past quarter.

Cisco and OpenAI Launch Shirudo, A New Project to Safeguard Agentic AI

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An industry-first open source project, Shirudo, helps systems defend themselves at machine speed

SAN JOSE, Calif., Nov. 23, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Cisco (NASDAQ: CSCO) and OpenAI today launched an open source effort to make infrastructure secure by default and able to defend itself against today's sophisticated cyber attacks. For too long, security has been a coat of paint. Cisco builds protection into the infrastructure itself, drawing on decades of building and protecting the systems the world depends on. OpenAI brings leading frontier AI research and insight into the attacks coming next. Together, they combine their insight to help anticipate AI-driven threats with the control to stop them.

Defenders must protect every part of their infrastructure, all the time. Attackers only need one machine that hasn't been patched. Shirudo is an important step toward closing that gap. The goal is infrastructure that is hardened from the moment it is built, keeps getting harder to attack, and can be restored to a known-good state if it is compromised.

"There is nothing more important than giving security teams the tools they need to match the speed of the AI-driven threats they are facing. We need self-defending infrastructure that acts at the speed of code, learns in real time, and shields itself until patches are safely rolled out," said Jeetu Patel, Cisco President and Chief Product Officer. "Today, we're proud to announce Shirudo with OpenAI, an important step towards that vision as we continue to collaborate with the wider community to solve the unique challenges of AI safety and security."

"We're giving defenders better models and tools to find and fix vulnerabilities faster, but finding a bug today doesn't mean you can patch every system tomorrow," said Greg Brockman, President and Co-founder of OpenAI. "We are partnering closely with Cisco on Shirudo to help teams protect the systems they need to keep running while they build and roll out a fix. So many teams rely on the same software that it makes sense to build these tools in the open. Now when one team finds a better way to protect a system, others don't have to start from scratch."

Shirudo: An Open Source Project from Cisco and OpenAIShirudo, Japanese for "shield," uses eBPF, a technology that runs small, custom programs inside the operating system kernel without changing its source code. With it, AI agents can find a vulnerability, build a shield, and test that it stops the attack without disrupting the service. Shields go live only when the evidence says they are safe. Every shield is cryptographically signed, so the host can verify it before it runs, even if an attacker has gained administrator access.

In testing against recent critical vulnerabilities "Java4shell" and the PHP flaw, Shirudo was able to write code that detected and blocked attempts to exploit both threats. By utilizing an agentic framework, Shirudo matches the speed of AI-driven attacks as soon as a vulnerability is identified. Learn more about Shirudo here.

Cisco and OpenAI are inviting developers, researchers, and enterprises to help shape what comes next. Contribute a test that reproduces a vulnerability, run a shield against a kernel build you use, or show us where it breaks legitimate traffic. To learn more about Shirudo, visit: https://github.com/shirudo-dev/shirudo.

About CiscoCisco (NASDAQ: CSCO) is the worldwide technology leader that is revolutionizing the way organizations connect and protect in the AI era. For more than 40 years, Cisco has securely connected the world. With its industry leading AI-powered solutions and services, Cisco enables its customers, partners and communities to unlock innovation, enhance productivity and strengthen digital resilience. With purpose at its core, Cisco remains committed to creating a more connected and inclusive future for all. Discover more on The Newsroom and follow us on X at @Cisco.

Cisco and the Cisco logo are trademarks or registered trademarks of Cisco and/or its affiliates in the U.S. and other countries. A listing of Cisco's trademarks can be found at http://www.cisco.com/go/trademarks. Third-party trademarks mentioned are the property of their respective owners. The use of the word 'partner' does not imply a partnership relationship between Cisco and any other company.

About OpenAI OpenAI is an AI research and deployment company with a mission to ensure that artificial general intelligence benefits all of humanity. We work toward that mission by developing more capable AI systems and making them available through products like ChatGPT and our API. People use these tools to learn, create and solve problems, while businesses and developers use them to build products and improve how they work.

‘Superintelligence is arriving’: Mark Zuckerberg’s AI chief warns humanity may lose control as the AI bubble refuses to pop

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer and the head of its Superintelligence Labs, posted on X on Sunday that “superintelligence is arriving.” He wrote that “the models are clearly going to be intelligent enough to automate all of AI research,” and that “it is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement.” He said the remaining obstacles are “no longer intelligence; they are domain-specific know-how, plumbing, and experimentation.”

“How humanity acts over the next year will have great consequence on what future world we live in,” Wang wrote.

Social media erupted over the latest warning from inside the AI thunderdome during a weekend when the AI bubble seemed to finally have popped — the day before it controversially reinflated again. Wang wasn’t even posting about Anthropic’s IPO or Jensen Huang’s resignation from Nvidia and sudden reinstatement on Monday morning.

He was riffing on just the latest historic math breakthrough with the help of generative AI: the claim by Levent Alpöge, a mathematician on Anthropic’s technical staff, of a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis made with Anthropic’s Claude. The 167-year-old problem about the distribution of prime numbers is one of the Clay Mathematics Institute’s $1 million Millennium Prize problems.

“It is hard to overstate the magnitude of discovery that we have just witnessed,” Wang wrote about the milestone, describing it as completed “with the help of an alien mind that we don’t understand.” He added that building an atomic bomb is “a homework problem compared to the Riemann Hypothesis.”

Wang’s warning came as Meta expects to spend $135 billion to $145 billion on capital expenditures this year, driven largely by AI infrastructure costs.

Wang’s post spread fast. The Next Web counted more than 8 million views in its first half day, and the reaction crossed the usual political lines. Jon Favreau, President Obama’s former director of speechwriting, called it “f—ing madness” and said the people Americans elected should be doing everything they can to stop the frontier labs “from handing over control of humanity to superintelligent machines.”

From the right, Megyn Kelly posted: “This is the head of Meta’s superintelligence lab. We’ve had similar warnings from Anthropic, the former head of Alignment at Open AI, not to mention Elon himself at X. How many more do we need until we garner the all-hands-on-deck reaction this deserves?”

Wang argued against fatalism. He wrote that he noticed “a particular form of lazy thinking within the AI industry”: the view that “ASI is inevitable and the only problem to solve is how to do it safely.” He added, “We should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI.”

After the backlash, he wrote, “What I am saying is this path is not inevitable,” and “Leaders and leadership matter more than ever.” He pointed to Meta’s decision to delay the launch of its Muse model for months and to Zuckerberg’s call for most computing power to go toward serving customers rather than toward AI that improves itself.

Zuckerberg made Wang the center of Meta’s AI push in June 2025, when Meta invested $14.3 billion in Wang’s former company, Scale AI, for a 49% stake. In January, Zuckerberg told investors that “this is going to be a big year for delivering personal superintelligence.” Meta has raised the bottom of its 2026 spending guidance at every quarterly report since, from $115 billion in January to $135 billion in October.

As for the Riemann Hypothesis, Anthropic’s Alpöge has said he plans to explain the AI-generated proof in talks and a written account. Until mathematicians read it, the Clay Institute’s $1 million stays unclaimed.

Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news.

All the endings (and a couple variations) for GTA 6 we've seen so far.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Obviously, massive spoilers follow for GTA 6 endings!

There are multiple endings to GTA 6, and while we haven't experienced them all personally here at PC Gamer, the gaming community has been sharing them around for anyone curious about what they may have missed.

The ending you get depends on the choices you make during the final GTA 6 story mission, but also depends on how you handled Jason and Lucia's romance and criminal profiles throughout the game, so seeing every version of the ending would mean not just playing the ending multiple times, but having different playthroughs of the game altogether.

There are a couple of endings that I don't consider "different" from each other but do have some differences leading up to them, which I'll explain below. There may also be additional endings we haven't seen yet, so we may update this guide in the future.

In the bridge endings, Jason and Lucia have defeated Mendez and his squad and gotten the hard drive back from Georgio's penthouse and escaped. They drive to Cal's place in Key Lento, but discover he has been compromised. They drive to the bridge, but the cops have set up a roadblock and have the couple trapped. Lucia wants to keep going, but Jason suggests they jump off the bridge and swim for it.

A choice comes up on the screen: "Swim For it" or "No Surrender."

Jason and Lucia try to run the roadblock and are shot to death by cops on the bridge. They die holding hands. In the credits, we see that their son Tomas lives happily with Lucia's aunt, her husband, and their children.

Jason and Lucia are shot to death by cops on the bridge. They die holding hands. In the credits, their lawyer, Abe, brings the money to Lucia's aunt. Tomas lives happily with Lucia's aunt, her husband, and their children in a much bigger house than the other No Surrender ending.

Choosing "Swim for it" presents an additional choice. When Jason gets out of the car, Lucia can choose to "Exit" the car with him, or change her mind by not choosing it.

Jason and Lucia decide to jump off the bridge and swim for it. They both exit the car without holding guns. Lucia jumps, but Jason doesn't jump and is arrested. Lucia signs a deal for immunity while Jason goes to prison. Lucia gets a job in a diner. Lucia takes Tomas to meet Jason in prison, but he can only see his son through a glass divider.

Jason and Lucia decide to jump off the bridge and swim for it. Jason exits the car with a gun, but Lucia changes her mind and stays in the car. The police open fire and Jason and Lucia are both shot to death. Tomas lives with Lucia's aunt, her husband, and their children.

Jason and Lucia decide to jump off the bridge and swim for it, Jason doesn't jump and is arrested. Two years later, Lucia uses the savings account to get Jason freed from prison. Lucia goes to the prison to pick up Jason. Jason meets Tomas and holds his hand.

If you don't have a strong relationship between Jason and Lucia, there is a different ending location: the base of Georgio's tower. In these endings, the couple never makes it to the bridge. You can also choose to break up the couple, either as Jason or as Lucia, as you begin the mission Protective Custody.

After the tower fight with Georgio's men, police confront the couple at the base of the tower. The police open fire and Jason pushes Lucia down. Jason is shot. Lucia has a choice to "Stay down" or "Defend Jason". She stays down and watches Jason die. Lucia is arrested, signs a deal, and visits Tomas at her aunt's house. Lucia then leaves Tomas with her aunt, takes out a gun, and continues her life of crime by preparing to rob a diner.

After the tower fight with Georgio's men, police confront them at the base of the tower. The police open fire and Jason pushes Lucia down. Jason is shot. Lucia chooses "Defend Jason" and fights the cops. They die together.

On the way down to ground level at the tower, Jason decides to run. Lucia is arrested and signs the deal, then lives with her aunt and Tomas while working in a diner. One night there's a knock on the door and she finds a bag of cash on the porch. We see Jason driving away with... Rocco the drug-sniffing dog from earlier in the game. Sure, that might as well happen.

How to get this ending (sourced by GamingwithTZ on YouTube):

Jason and Lucia make a deal with Mendez after defeating him. Once they have the hard drive, they're surrounded by cops at the bottom of the tower. Mendez arrives and takes them away in his car, then drives them to the swamp. Later in the credits, we see two skulls in the swamp, side-by-side, with a bullet hole in each.

Note: You may need a specific criminal profile for this ending. I tried to take the deal (as Lucia) in one of my ending playthroughs, but Jason shot Mendez anyway. I had both Jason and Lucia at "Professional" level criminal profiles. This ending was not available to me. Speculation, but this ending may only be available if you don't have "Professional" as your criminal profiles.

There are potentially (and almost certainly) more endings to GTA 6. There are still a few combinations of all the different variables, including your romance level, criminal profile level, amount of money saved, and the choices you make during the final mission, that we haven't seen yet.

We'll update this guide if the community finds more endings.

How to Win Big Betting on Jensen Huang? Listen to Trump

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Just days ago, Jensen Huang’s chances of returning as Nvidia CEO looked increasingly slim, at least according to prediction markets. Earlier this month, Huang resigned from the AI hardware giant following reports of multiple extramarital affairs, including one with a woman alleged to have ties to Chinese intelligence services. As more reports emerged about Huang’s alleged bad behavior, Nvidia’s board insisted it had no plans to reinstate him. By early Sunday morning on the East Coast, traders on Polymarket’s US platform put his chances of returning at just 10 percent. But by Monday, Huang was back in charge.

The swings in Huang’s odds on prediction markets offer a window into how his unlikely comeback unfolded and who saw it coming. The New York Times reported that President Trump played a crucial role in convincing the board to bring Huang back. Traders who paid attention to what Trump and his allies wanted appear to have fared much better than those who did not.

On Sunday evening in Washington, Huang’s odds on Polymarket US jumped six percentage points in just 40 minutes after Trump published a Truth Social missive calling for Huang’s reinstatement. The president said he wanted to help revive the stock market, which had plunged following Huang’s scandal, a sign of Nvidia’s enormous influence over the AI industry and broader US economy.

According to the prediction market intelligence firm Betting Intelligence, odds on both Kalshi and Polymarket’s markets largely tracked with public signals about the likelihood of Huang’s return. They rose when Huang said he was open to returning, fell when the board dismissed the possibility, and climbed again when President Trump threw his support behind the former CEO. But there were several large trades that could not be tied to public developments.

Betting Intelligence and the prediction market forensics firm MSR Decode identified clusters of trades on Huang’s return that did not appear to be driven by news or public commentary. Kalshi and Polymarket declined to comment on whether they are currently investigating potential insider trading in markets related to Huang’s return.

Despite these anomalies, Betting Intelligence CEO Dan Zimmermann says the Huang-related markets showed fewer signs of potentially suspicious activity than usual. “The group that could have known the board’s decision in advance was small. So the market was mostly headline chasers and longshot buyers,” he says.

For traders who profited from Huang’s comeback, the lesson is clear: Don’t discount Trump.

Isaac Rose-Berman, a research and policy fellow at the American Institute for Boys & Men and professional sports bettor, says he made approximately $10,000 wagering on Huang’s return on Kalshi. He placed his trade after the Nvidia chief said on X that he was open to reclaiming his old job. Rose-Berman says that he figured that the board would consider Huang’s return if it looked likely to improve the company’s stock performance. (Never mind the concerns about national security or Huang allegedly threatening employees that he would “pee in your brain.”) “It’s 2026 and Trump is president, scandal schmandal,” Rose-Berman says. “Should’ve bet more.”

A pseudonymous prediction market trader known as Domer tells WIRED he cleared approximately $600,000 by placing an early wager last week on Huang’s return. He wasn’t focused explicitly on Trump’s desires, but he says his understanding of power dynamics ultimately guided his approach. “Boards can be very malleable,” he tells WIRED.

Other prediction market “sharps” say that commentary from Trumpworld was the strongest signal available about Huang’s return. As some bettors noted, there was a notable opportunity for profit after Trump suggested to a PBS NewsHour reporter that Huang would be back in power soon, but before Nvidia officially announced his reinstatement. “The key was looking at Trump’s support and the recent performance of the stock market,” says Kalshi trader Caleb Davies.

Aaron Courtney, a data analyst and cofounder of a prediction market analytics firm, says he took a loss on his Huang position. Looking back, he says his mistake was not paying attention to what Trump and other administration officials said over the weekend. “Trump saying he hoped Nvidia would bring Jensen back would be the clearest signal,” he says.

Disneyland’s Fantasmic Once Again Went Up In Flames, And I’m Starting To Worry About The Show’s Future

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Turns Out, When You Play A Disney Princess They Let You Sleep In Walt's Disneyland Apartment

Billie From Hexed Has Just Landed At Disney Parks, But The Directors Already Have Bigger Ideas

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Disneyland live entertainment is few and far between as of late, which is a shame considering the Anaheim theme park used to boast some of the best shows. On November 21st, one of the last and most beloved nighttime shows, Fantasmic, went up in flames for the second time in three short years. With two incidents and Disney’s desire to cut back on live entertainment, I’m really starting to worry about the future of the show.

The fire reportedly broke out during Fantasmic’s second performance of the night shortly before the show’s finale. While the Mark Twain finale was able to go on, flames can be seen behind the boat on the small stage set up on Tom Saywer Island. Guests at the park were quick to pull out their phones and film the growing flames as the Mark Twain, full of classic Disney characters, made its way around the river. Take a look:

Here’s the Mark Twain Riverboat running during Fantasmic last night with the fire behind it. This happened during the 10:30PM show https://t.co/bMcOZ6z0PtNovember 22, 2026

The last two minutes of the show were canceled, and guests were asked to vacate the viewing area while the fire department handled the blaze. Thankfully, the fire was small compared to the 2023 incident that turned the Maleficent dragon animatronic into a giant fireball and forever changed the show. There’s been no word on the extent of the damage this time around, but considering the show was back Sunday night it safe to assume that nothing was affected and no cast members were harmed, thankfully.

Two fiery incidents are definitely a cause for concern, and with Disneyland executives’ history of scaling back entertainment to cut costs, I can’t help but worry about the show’s fate. After all, Fantasmic boasts a large cast of incredible performers and several floating units. Plus water screens, projectors, and fireworks. I’m no expert, but I’m willing to bet it’s one of the most costly attractions at Disneyland. Not to mention, the show is still feeling the loss of the Maleficent Dragon, which makes the battle between Mickey and Maleficent lackluster.

While I don’t think Disneyland would end the show without also announcing a replacement, I do think Disney fans should prepare themselves. After all, Disney fans aren’t known for taking new changes well. Just look at the uproar that happened when it was announced Dinosaur would close to make room for a new Indiana Jones attraction at Disney World.

Disneyland has been having a rough go at things this month. On the first day of the holiday season, the Anaheim theme park’s iconic fake snow was accidentally turned on during a rainstorm, creating a mess. Back in October, Frontierland experienced widespread flooding. With park tickets being so expensive, it’s unfortunate to see cracks in Disneyland’s magic, but not even the mouse can control the weather or unexpected fires.
